1
follows directly from Proposition
A1
, as
Q_{\infty}
takes values in
H^{1}((0,1))
a.s.

B.3
Proposition
3

To begin, we note that the
L^{2}
norm is weaker than the Sobolev norm, that is
d_{2}(f,g)\leq d_{1,2}(f,g)
. As a result, we have from Theorem
1
that
d_{2}(Q_{N},Q_{\infty})\to 0
a.s. As
Q_{N}
and
Q_{\infty}
take values in
H^{1}((0,1))
, Proposition
A2
implies that
Q_{N}
and
Q_{\infty}
are a.e. equal to continuous bounded functions on
(0,1)
a.s., which we can extend to
[0,1]
. We can then apply Proposition
2
, which gives

d_{2}(Q_{N}^{\dagger},Q_{\infty}^{\dagger})\leq d_{2}(Q_{N},Q_{\infty})
, and thus

d_{2}(Q_{N}^{\dagger},Q_{\infty}^{\dagger})\to 0
a.s.
Furthermore, the increasing rearrangement variant of Theorem
A2
implies that
Q_{N}^{\dagger}
and
Q_{\infty}^{\dagger}
are both in
L^{2}(H^{1}(0,1))
, and thus take values in
H^{1}((0,1))
a.s.

B.4
Corollary
2

As
P_{N}\to P_{\infty}
weakly a.s., we can directly apply
Aldous et al. [1985, Lemma 8.2(b)]
to show the asymptotic exchangeability of the sequence. For the second part, we assume
n=0
so the notation is simpler, but this is just a matter of relabelling the indices. We begin by writing

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{N}(y):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}\left(Y_{i}\leq y\right)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}\left(Q_{i-1}(V_{i})\leq y\right)

where
V_{i}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{U}(0,1)
. We then have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{P}_{N}(y)\mid\mathcal{F}_{N-1}]=\frac{N-1}{N}\mathbb{P}_{N-1}+\frac{1}{N}P_{N-1}(y)

which follows as
\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}\left(Q_{N}(v)\leq y\right)\,dv
is precisely
P_{N}(y)
.

Following the proof of
[
Berti et al., 2004
, Theorem 2.2]
, for any continuous and bounded
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
, we define
U_{N}=f(Y_{N+1})
.
We then define the martingale

Z_{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\frac{U_{i}-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]}{i+1},

which is bounded in
L^{2}
, so
(Z_{N})_{N\geq 1}
converges a.s. Kronecker’s lemma then gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\left(U_{i}-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\right)\to 0\quad\text{a.s.}

As a result, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}U_{i}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\quad\text{a.s.}

Note that the left term is exactly
\mathbb{P}_{N}[f]
, where
P[f]=\int fdP
. We also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[U_{N}\mid\mathcal{F}_{N-1}]=P_{N}[f]\to P_{\infty}[f]\quad\text{a.s.}

as
P_{N}
converges weakly to
P_{\infty}
a.s., so
\mathbb{P}_{N}[f]\to P_{\infty}[f]
a.s. through a Césaro means argument. The above holds a.s. for the function
f=e^{ity}
for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, so
Berti et al. [2006, Theorem 2.6]
gives us
\mathbb{P}_{N}\to P_{\infty}
weakly a.s.

B.5
Theorem
2

The proof of this theorem consists of two parts. First we verify the conditions of Theorem
A3
to obtain asymptotic tightness of
\sqrt{N}S_{N}
. Then it suffices to check marginal convergence on
\mathcal{F}
using the Lindeberg-Feller CLT; that is, we show that there is weak convergence to the requisite multivariate normal distribution for any finite collection of points.

Theorem A5
.

The sequence of functions
\sqrt{N}S_{N}
is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}((0,1))
.

Proof.

We will verify the conditions of Theorem
A3
. Our semimetric space is
(\mathcal{F}=(0,1),d)
, where
d(u_{1},u_{2})=|u_{1}-u_{2}|^{1/2}
. Clearly, this semimetric space is totally bounded.

First define
Z_{Ni}(u)=\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})
for all
N
and
i\geq N
. A trivial envelope function for
Z_{Ni}
is
F_{Ni}=\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}
. We need to verify the Lindeberg condition

\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}F_{Ni}\mathbbm{1}\{F_{Ni}>\eta\}\rightarrow 0

for every
\eta>0
. Since
F_{Ni}<c/\sqrt{N}
, we will have
F_{Ni}<\eta
for all sufficiently large
n
. Thus, the Lindeberg condition holds.

Next we need

\sup_{d(u_{1},u_{2})<\delta_{N}}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1})-Z_{Ni}(u_{2})\right\}^{2}\rightarrow 0

for every
\delta_{N}\downarrow 0
. Let
u_{1}>u_{2}
with
d(u_{1},u_{2})<\delta_{N}
. Note that
Z_{Ni}(u)
is non-decreasing with
\lim_{u\downarrow 0}Z_{Ni}(u)=0
and
\lim_{u\uparrow 1}Z_{Ni}(u)=\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}
. So

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1})-Z_{Ni}(u_{2})\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1})-Z_{Ni}(u_{2})\right\}

\displaystyle\leq N\alpha_{i}^{2}(u_{1}-u_{2})

\displaystyle<N\alpha_{i}^{2}\delta_{N}^{2}.

So

\sup_{d(u_{1},u_{2})<\delta_{N}}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1})-Z_{Ni}(u_{2})\right\}^{2}<N\delta_{N}^{2}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}.

Since
\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq c^{2}
, the right-hand side of the above display tends to zero for any
\delta_{N}\downarrow 0
.

Finally, we need to verify the bracketing entropy integral condition. For all sufficiently large
N
, we will have
N\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq 2c^{2}
. Given
\varepsilon>0
, choose a partition
0=u_{0}<u_{1}<\ldots<u_{M}=1
such that
u_{j}-u_{j-1}<\varepsilon^{2}/(2c^{2})
for every
j
. The number of points in the partition can be chosen to be smaller than a constant times
1/\varepsilon^{2}
. Then

\mathbb{E}\sup_{u_{j-1}\leq s,t<u_{j}}|Z_{Ni}(s)-Z_{Ni}(t)|^{2}<\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}(u_{j})-Z_{Ni}(u_{j-1})]<\frac{N\alpha_{i}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2c^{2}},

where we have taken
Z_{Ni}(0)=0
and
Z_{Ni}(1)=\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}
for notational convenience. We deduce that for all sufficiently large
N
,

\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\sup_{u_{j-1}\leq s,t<u_{j}}|Z_{Ni}(s)-Z_{Ni}(t)|^{2}<\varepsilon^{2}.

In other words,
\mathfrak{N}_{[]}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},L^{2,N})\lesssim{\varepsilon^{-2}}
, which verifies the entropy condition. Thus, Theorem
A3
implies that

-\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}(Z_{Ni}(u)-\mathbb{E}Z_{Ni}(u))=\sqrt{N}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i}))

is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

Proposition A4
.

Consider a vector
\mathbf{S}_{N}(\mathbf{u})=[S_{N}(u_{1}),\ldots,S_{N}(u_{d})]^{T}
where each
u_{j}\in(0,1)
. We then have

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\mathbf{S}_{N}(\mathbf{u})\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},a^{2}\Sigma)

where
\Sigma_{j,k}=\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}-u_{j}u_{k}
.

Proof.

We will use the Lindeberg-Feller CLT
[
Van der Vaart, 2000
, Proposition 2.27]
, which we state below for convenience. For each
N
, let
\{Z_{N,N},\ldots,Z_{N,m_{N}}\}
be independent r.v.s with finite variances that satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\left(\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}]^{2}\right)\to\sigma^{2}

and the Lindeberg condition

\displaystyle\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left(|Z_{Ni}|>\varepsilon\right)]\to 0

for every
\varepsilon>0
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}(Z_{Ni}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}])\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}).

We take
m_{N}
to be any sequence such that
N/m_{N}=o(1)
. Consider an arbitrary vector
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and we study the convergence of

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\,\mathbf{t}^{T}\mathbf{S}_{N}(\mathbf{u})=\sqrt{N}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\,t_{j}\,S_{N}(u_{j})=\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\sqrt{N}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\,t_{j}\,\alpha_{i}(u_{j}-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{j},V_{i})).

For
i\geq N
, set

Z_{Ni}=\sqrt{N}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\,t_{j}\,\alpha_{i}H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{j},V_{i}),

which has expectation
\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}
. We have

|Z_{Ni}|\leq a\frac{\sqrt{N}}{i}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|t_{j}|\leq\frac{a}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|t_{j}|.

So for every
\varepsilon>0
,
|Z_{Ni}|<\varepsilon
eventually, which establishes the Lindeberg condition. It remains to study the limiting variance. Using the integral test, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}]^{2}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\frac{N}{i^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{\mathbb{E}\left[H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{j},V_{i})\,H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{k},V_{i})\right]-u_{j}u_{k}\}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\frac{N}{i^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u_{j},u_{k})-u_{j}u_{k}\}.

Taking
\rho^{2}\to 1
gives
C_{\rho^{2}}(u_{j},u_{k})\to\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}
as discussed in Section
A.5
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\left(\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}_{Ni}]-\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}]^{2}\right)-a^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}-u_{j}u_{k}\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left|\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\frac{a^{2}N}{i^{2}}\left[C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u_{j},u_{k})-\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}\right]\right|+a^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}-u_{j}u_{k}\}\left|\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\frac{N}{i^{2}}-1\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left|C_{\rho_{N}^{2}}(u_{j},u_{k})-\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}\right|\left|\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\frac{a^{2}N}{i^{2}}\right|+a^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}-u_{j}u_{k}\}\left|\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\frac{N}{i^{2}}-1\right|

\displaystyle+a^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}-u_{j}u_{k}\}\left|\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}\frac{N}{i^{2}}\right|.

The first inequality uses the triangle inequality. The first and second terms in the last expression are
o(1)
. The third term is
O(N/m_{N})
, which is
o(1)
by construction. Thus, we have

\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\left(\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}_{Ni}]-\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}]^{2}\right)\to\sigma^{2}=a^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{\min\{u_{j},u_{k}\}-u_{j}u_{k}\}

and we can apply the Lindeberg-Feller CLT to obtain

\sum_{i=N}^{m_{N}}\sqrt{N}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\,t_{j}\,\alpha_{i}(u_{j}-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{j},V_{i}))\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}).

The final step is to show that the tail sums from
m_{N}+1
onwards become asymptotically negligible. We do this by checking that
\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}\text{Var}[Z_{Ni}]\to 0
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}\text{Var}[Z_{Ni}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}a^{2}\frac{N}{i^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\{C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u_{j},u_{k})-u_{j}u_{k}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}a^{2}\frac{N}{i^{2}}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}t_{j}t_{k}\right|

\displaystyle\to 0.

Now the Cramér-Wold device completes the proof.
∎

B.6
Theorem
3

To begin, we assume that
Y_{1:n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}P^{*}
which has quantile function
Q^{*}
satisfying Assumption
5
. Let
\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
denote the filtration where
\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\sigma\left(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{i}\right)
. For now, we do not need to consider
Y_{n+1:\infty}
arising from predictive resampling.
To start, we construct an almost supermartingale as in Theorem
A4
arising from Algorithm
3
:

\displaystyle\left(Q^{*}(u)-Q_{n}(u)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))^{2}+2\alpha_{n}(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))\left(H_{\rho_{n}}\left(u,V_{n}\right)-u\right)

\displaystyle+\alpha_{n}^{2}\left(H_{\rho_{n}}\left(u,V_{n})\right)-u\right)^{2}.

where for shorthand we write
V_{n}=P_{n-1}(Y_{n})
. We note that
|H_{\rho}(u,v)-u|\leq 1
.
Integrating with respect to
u
, we have

\displaystyle d_{2}^{2}\left(Q^{*},Q_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{2}^{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1},Q^{*})+2\alpha_{n}\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))\left(H_{\rho_{n}}\left(u,V_{n}\right)-u\right)du+\alpha_{n}^{2}.

Now comes the key step due to the rearrangement. By Assumption
5
, both
Q^{*}
and
Q_{0}
are Lipschitz continuous and thus bounded on
[0,1]
. Since
H_{\rho}(u,v)-u
is bounded on
u\in[0,1]
,
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
is bounded on
[0,1]
. We can thus apply Proposition
A3
, which gives

d_{2}^{2}(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*})\leq d_{2}^{2}(Q_{n},Q^{*}),

so we have

\displaystyle d_{2}^{2}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n},Q^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{2}^{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1},Q^{*})+2\alpha_{n}\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))\left(H_{\rho_{n}}\left(u,V_{n}\right)-u\right)du+\alpha_{n}^{2}.

Taking the conditional expectation gives us

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]\leq L_{n-1}+2\alpha_{n}\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))\left(K_{n}\left(u\right)-u\right)du+\alpha_{n}^{2},

where we write
L_{n}=d_{2}^{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{n},Q^{*})
and

K_{n}(u)=\int H_{\rho_{n}}(u,P_{n-1}(y))\,p^{*}(y)\,dy.

We now subtract and add terms to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]\leq L_{n-1}-2\alpha_{n}\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))(u-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)))\,du+\alpha_{n}^{2}+2\alpha_{n}\zeta_{n}

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{n}=\int(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))(K_{n}\left(u\right)-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)))\,du.

(A10)

It is not too hard to see that

\displaystyle T(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}):=\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))(u-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)))du\geq 0,

where the positivity can be seen by applying
Q^{*}
, which is monotonically increasing, to each term in
u-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))
, giving

\displaystyle u-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u))\geq 0\implies Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)\geq 0.

To get a handle on
\zeta_{n}
, Cauchy-Schwartz gives us

|\zeta_{n}|\leq\sqrt{L_{n-1}}\sqrt{\int(K_{n}\left(u\right)-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)))^{2}\,du}

and applying
\sqrt{x}\leq x+1
gives

|\zeta_{n}|\leq(L_{n-1}+1)\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}

where we write

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}={\int(K_{n}\left(u\right)-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)))^{2}\,du}

(A11)

This gives the original inequality as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]\leq\left(1+2\alpha_{n}\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}\right)L_{n}-2\alpha_{n}T(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger})+\alpha_{n}^{2}+2\alpha_{n}\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}.

We now seek to apply Theorem
A4
with
B_{i}=2\alpha_{i}\sqrt{\kappa_{i}}
,
C_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{2}+2\alpha_{i}\sqrt{\kappa_{i}}
and
D_{i}=2\alpha_{i}T(Q_{i-1}^{\dagger})
, where all terms are positive.
A sufficient condition for
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}B_{i}<\infty
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}C_{i}<\infty
a.s. is if

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\sqrt{\kappa_{i}}<\infty\quad\text{a.s. }

which we now prove.

B.6.1
Controlling
\kappa_{n}

We begin by upper bounding
\kappa_{i}
, which intuitively measures how far the copula kernel
H_{\rho}(u,v)
is from the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}\left(v\leq u\right)
(averaged over
u,v
). The result is simple to state, but the proof is surprisingly quite involved, and requires specific properties of the bivariate Gaussian copula. The difficulty of the proof arises from the copula’s dependence on
P_{n}
, as we require bounds independent of
P_{n}
and
P^{*}
. This
highlights that while the bivariate copula is very useful for attaining the martingale and coherence required for Bayesian inference, its adaptivity makes it harder to study its properties for estimation.

Lemma A10
.

Let
\kappa_{n}
be defined as in (
A11
), and suppose
\rho_{n}\to 1
. For sufficiently large
n
, we have that
\kappa_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}\leq K(1-\rho_{n}^{2})^{1/4}

for some positive finite constant
K
.

Proof.

To start, we can write

\displaystyle P^{*}(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}(u))

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}(y\leq Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}(u))\,dP^{*}(y)=\int\mathbbm{1}(P_{n-1}(y)\leq u)\,dP^{*}(y).

This gives

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\left[\int(H_{\rho_{n}}(u,P_{n-1}(y))-\mathbbm{1}(P_{n-1}(y)\leq u))\,dP^{*}(y)\right]^{2}\,du

\displaystyle\leq\int\int(H_{\rho_{n}}(u,P_{n-1}(y))-\mathbbm{1}(P_{n-1}(y)\leq u))^{2}\,du\,dP^{*}(y).

Let us write the inner integral as a function of a general
\rho,v\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle U(v;\rho):=\int\int(H_{\rho}(u,v)-\mathbbm{1}(v\leq u))^{2}\,du.

Fortunately, we can control
U(v;\rho)
by taking
\rho\to 1
. To see this, note that

\displaystyle\int(H_{\rho}(u,v)-\mathbbm{1}(v\leq u))^{2}\,du=\int H^{2}_{\rho}(u,v)\,du-2\int_{v}^{1}H_{\rho}(u,v)\,du+\int_{v}^{1}\,du.

(A12)

A change of variables with
z_{u}=\Phi^{-1}(u)
gives

H_{\rho}(u,v)=\Phi(a+bz_{u})

where

\displaystyle a=-\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}z_{v},\quad b=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}

where
z_{v}=\Phi^{-1}(v)
. We now require some integrals of Gaussian CDF and density terms, which can be found in
Owen [1980]
.
For the first term of (
A12
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Phi(a+bz_{u})^{2}\phi(z_{u})\,dz_{u}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}};\rho=\frac{b^{2}}{1+b^{2}}\right)

where

\Phi_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\rho)
is the standard bivariate normal CDF evaluated at
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
with correlation
\rho
. This follows from codes (20,010.4) and (3.5) from
Owen [1980]
.

For the second term of (
A12
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{z_{v}}^{\infty}\Phi(a+bz_{u})\,\phi(z_{u})\,dz_{u}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Phi(a+bz_{u})\,\phi(z_{u})\,dz_{u}-\int_{-\infty}^{z_{v}}\Phi(a+bz_{u})\,\phi(z_{u})\,dz_{u}

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}}\right)-\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},z_{v};\rho=\frac{-b}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},-z_{v};\rho=\frac{b}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}}\right)

where the second line comes from codes (10,010.1) and (10,010.1) from
Owen [1980]
, and the third line comes from the identity
\Phi(a)-\Phi_{2}(a,b;\rho)=\Phi_{2}(a,-b;-\rho)
. The final term of (
A12
) is simply
1-v
. Putting this together, we have that

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}};\rho=\frac{b^{2}}{1+b^{2}}\right)-2\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},-z_{v};\rho=\frac{b}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}}\right)+(1-v)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{-\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v},\frac{-\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v};\rho=\frac{1}{2-\rho^{2}}\right)-2\Phi_{2}\left(\frac{-\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v},-z_{v};\rho=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}\right)+(1-v)

We will first write the above in terms of the tail probability of a bivariate normal distribution,
L(h,k;\rho)=P(x>h,y>k;\rho)
, which is easier to bound and satisfies

\displaystyle L(h,k;\rho)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}(-h,-k;\rho)

This gives us

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v},\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v};\frac{1}{2-\rho^{2}}\right)-2L\left(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v},z_{v};\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}\right)+(1-v)

\displaystyle=L(h,h;\bar{\rho}^{2})-2L(h,z_{v};\bar{\rho})+Q(z_{v})

where we define
h=\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}z_{v}
,
\bar{\rho}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}
, and
Q(z)=1-\Phi(z)
is the Gaussian tail probability.
We first show that this upper bound is symmetric around
z_{v}=0
. Using the identity

L(h,k;\rho)=1-\Phi(h)-\Phi(k)+L(-h,-k;\rho)

from the above, we can see that
U(v;\rho)=U(1-v;\rho)
noting that
z_{1-v}=-z_{v}
.
As a result, we just need to bound
U(v;\rho)
for
z_{v}\geq 0
(i.e.
v\geq 0.5
).

We note that
L(h,h;\bar{\rho}^{2})\leq L(h,h;\bar{\rho})
as
L
is increasing with
\bar{\rho}
, and furthermore
L(h,z_{v};\bar{\rho})\geq L(z_{v},z_{v};\bar{\rho})
as
z_{v}\geq h
. This then gives us

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)

\displaystyle\leq L(h,h;\bar{\rho})-2L(z_{v},z_{v};\bar{\rho})+Q(z_{v}).

One can show that

\frac{\partial L(h,h;\bar{\rho})}{\partial h}=-2\Phi(-\theta h)\phi(h)

where
\theta=\sqrt{\frac{1-\bar{\rho}}{1+\bar{\rho}}}
. A Taylor expansion gives

\displaystyle L(z_{v},z_{v};\bar{\rho})

\displaystyle=L(h,h;\bar{\rho})-2\Phi(-\theta\tilde{h})\,\phi(\tilde{h})\,(z_{v}-h)

\displaystyle\geq L(h,h;\bar{\rho})-2\Phi(-\theta{h})\,\phi({h})\,(z_{v}-h),

where
h\leq\tilde{h}\leq z_{v}
. The second line follows as the function
\Phi(-\theta h)\,\phi(h)
is monotonically decreasing with
h
for
h\geq 0
. This then implies

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)\leq 2\Phi(-\theta{h})\,\phi({h})(z_{v}-h)+Q(z_{v})-L(z_{v},z_{v};\bar{\rho}).

We can upper bound the first term as

\displaystyle\phi(h)(z_{v}-h)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}{\rho}-1\right)\,\phi(h)\,h\leq\phi(1)\left(\frac{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}{\rho}-1\right)

and
\Phi(-\theta h)\leq 0.5
. For the second term, we can compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{v}}(Q(z_{v})-L(z_{v},z_{v};\bar{\rho}))=[2\Phi(-\theta z_{v})-1]\,\phi(z_{v}).

The derivative is always non-positive for
z_{v}\geq 0
and is equal to 0 at
z_{v}=0
, so the maximum value must be

\displaystyle Q(0)-L(0,0;\bar{\rho})=\frac{1}{2}-\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\arcsin(\bar{\rho})\right).

Together, this implies

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)

\displaystyle\leq\phi(1)\left(\frac{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}{\rho}-1\right)+\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\arcsin\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi(1)\left(\frac{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}{\rho}-1\right)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\arctan\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}

For
x\geq 0
, we have
\arctan x\leq x
as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\arctan x=\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\leq\frac{d}{dx}x=1

and
\arctan x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}
at
x^{\prime}=0
. We thus have

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)

\displaystyle\leq\phi(1)\left(\frac{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}{\rho}-1\right)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}

Finally, for sufficiently large
\rho
we have that

\frac{\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}{\rho}-1\leq\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}.

This follows because the roots of
g(\rho)={\sqrt{2-\rho^{2}}}/{\rho}-1-\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
occur at
\rho\approx 0.7184
and
\rho=1
,
g(\rho)
is continuous on
\rho\in(0,1]
and
g(\rho)
is negative for some point in between the two roots. For
\rho
sufficiently close to
1
, we thus have

\displaystyle U(v;\rho)\leq K^{2}\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}

(A13)

for some finite and positive
K
. If
\rho_{n}\to 1
, this thus gives

\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}\leq K(1-\rho_{n}^{2})^{1/4}

for sufficiently large
n
.
∎

B.6.2
Almost supermartingale

If
\alpha_{n}=a(n+1)^{-1}
, Lemma
A10
implies that setting

1-\rho_{n}^{2}=O(n^{-k})

for some
k>0
is sufficient for
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\sqrt{\kappa_{i}}<\infty
.
Given Assumptions
3
and
4
, we have that
L_{n}
is an almost supermartingale, so we can apply Theorem
A4
. This implies
L_{n}\to L_{\infty}<\infty
a.s. and more importantly,

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}T(Q_{i-1}^{\dagger})<\infty\quad\text{a.s.}

As
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}=\infty
, one can verify that the above implies

\liminf_{n}T(Q_{n}^{\dagger})=0\quad\text{a.s.}

which implies there is a subsequence
n_{j}
on which
\lim_{n_{j}\to\infty}T(Q_{n_{j}}^{\dagger})=0
.
We will now use the fact that
Q^{*}
is
M
-Lipschitz on
[0,1]
from Assumption
5
, which gives

\displaystyle|Q^{*}(u)-Q(u)|\leq M|u-P^{*}(Q(u))|

for all
u\in[0,1]
. We thus have

\displaystyle T(Q_{n}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n}(u))(u-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n}(u)))\,du

\displaystyle=\int\,|Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n}(u)|\,|u-P^{*}(Q^{\dagger}_{n}(u))|\,du

\displaystyle\geq M^{-1}\int\,(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n}(u))^{2}\,du\,=M^{-1}L_{n}

where the second equality follows from the positivity of
T(Q)
. Applying this to the subsequence
n_{j}
gives
L_{n_{j}}\leq MT(Q_{n_{j}}^{\dagger})
, so
L_{n_{j}}\to 0
along this subsequence. Since
L_{n}\to L_{\infty}
a.s., we have
L_{\infty}=0
a.s.

B.7
Theorem
4

Consider now the same setting as the proof of Theorem
3
.
To begin, we first show that
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
for each
n
. We note that the update function
(u-H_{\rho}(u,v))
has continuous partial derivative in
u
for all
v\in[0,1]
(as shown in (
A9
)). As a result,
Q_{1}
is a sum of a Lipschitz function
Q_{0}
and a continuously differentiable function, so
Q_{1}\in H^{1}((0,1))
, and
Q_{1}^{\dagger}\in H^{1}((0,1))
by Theorem
A2
. Repeating the argument gives
Q_{n}^{\dagger}\in H^{1}((0,1))
, thus satisfying Assumptions
1
and
2
.

We now extend the probability space. For each
n
, define
Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}
as the random function with realizations in
H^{1}((0,1))
arising from Algorithm
4
starting from
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
. The existence of
Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}\in H^{1}((0,1))
is guaranteed by Theorem
1
. There are a few possible constructions of this space, for example we can let
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots
be i.i.d. r.v.s from
P^{*}
, and independently let
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots
be i.i.d. r.v.s from
\mathcal{U}(0,1)
. We can then define the following:

\displaystyle S_{n}(u)=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}[u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})],\quad Q_{n\infty}(u)=Q_{n}^{\dagger}(u)+S_{n}(u).

Another option is to let
V_{n,n+1},V_{n,n+2},\ldots
be distinct independent sequences of uniform r.v.s for each
n
. Either way, this does not affect the next step, as the distribution of
Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}
for each
n
is unchanged.

To show posterior consistency, we apply Markov’s inequality which gives

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[d^{2}_{2}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right].

As
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
and
Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}
are essentially bounded by Proposition
A2
,
we can apply Proposition
2
which gives
d^{2}_{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty},Q^{*})\leq d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)
, and thus

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right].

Applying the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle E\left[d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(d_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)+d_{2}\left(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*}\right)\right)^{2}\mid Y_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right]+2d_{2}\left(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right]+d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*}\right).

To compute the first term, we have

\displaystyle d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{N},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)=d^{2}_{2}(Q_{N-1},Q_{n}^{\dagger})+d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{N},Q_{N-1}\right)-2\int\left(Q_{N}(u)-Q_{N-1}(u)\right)\left(Q_{N-1}(u)-Q_{n}^{\dagger}(u)\right)\,du.

As
Q_{N}(u)
is a martingale, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{N},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)\mid Y_{1:N-1}\right]

\displaystyle=d^{2}_{2}(Q_{N-1},Q_{n}^{\dagger})+\alpha_{N}^{2}\int\,\int\left(u-H_{\rho_{N}}(u,v)\right)^{2}\,du\,dv

\displaystyle\leq d^{2}_{2}(Q_{N-1},Q_{n}^{\dagger})+\alpha_{N}^{2}.

Iterating further, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d^{2}_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}=O(n^{-1}).

Let us consider the second term. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty},Q_{n}^{\dagger}\right)\mid Y_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{E\left[d^{2}_{2}(Q_{n\infty},Q_{n}^{\dagger})\mid Y_{1:n}\right]}

\displaystyle=O(n^{-1/2}).

Putting this together, we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}\left(Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger},Q^{*}\right)\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[O(n^{-1})+2O(n^{-1/2})\,d_{2}(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*})+d^{2}_{2}(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*})\right].

(A14)

We thus have the above going to 0 as long as
d^{2}_{2}(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*})\to 0

P^{*}
-a.s., which follows from Theorem
3
.

B.8
Theorem
5

This proof fortunately recycles many steps from the proof of Theorem
3
, with additional steps inspired by
Aboubacar and Thiam [2014]
. Let us begin again with the almost supermartingale construction:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]\leq\left(1+2\alpha_{n}\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}\right)L_{n-1}-2\alpha_{n}T(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger})+\alpha_{n}^{2}+2\alpha_{n}\sqrt{\kappa_{n}},

where
\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}=O(n^{-k/4})
, which is the error term controlled by the bandwidth with
k>0
. From the Lipschitz condition in Assumption
5
, we again have

\displaystyle T(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger})\geq M^{-1}L_{n-1}.

Putting this together, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]

\displaystyle\leq\left[1-2\alpha_{n}\left(M^{-1}-\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}\right)\right]L_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}^{2}+2\alpha_{n}\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}

\displaystyle=\left[1-2\alpha_{n}(M^{-1}+O(n^{-k/4}))\right]L_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}^{2}+2\alpha_{n}\sqrt{\kappa_{n}}.

Premultiplying by
n^{\delta}
for
\delta<1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[n^{\delta}L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]

\displaystyle\leq\left[1-2an^{-1}(M^{-1}+O(n^{-k/4}))\right][1+\delta n^{-1}+O(n^{-2})](n-1)^{\delta}L_{n-1}

\displaystyle+O\left(n^{-(2-\delta)}\right)+O(n^{\delta-1-k/4})

where we have used the fact that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{\delta}=1+\frac{\delta}{n}+O(n^{-2})

which is also used in
Aboubacar and Thiam [2014]
.
Simplifying, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[n^{\delta}L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]

\displaystyle\leq\left[1-n^{-1}(2aM^{-1}-\delta+{o}(1))\right](n-1)^{\delta}L_{n-1}+O\left(n^{-(2-\delta)}\right)+O(n^{\delta-1-k/4})

If we choose
a
such that
a>\frac{M\delta}{2}
, then
2aM^{-1}-\delta+o(1)>0
eventually for sufficiently large
n
. If we further assume
k>4
, then the last terms is
O(n^{-(2-\delta)})
, so we just need
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-(2-\delta)}<\infty
for the bounded variance condition, which holds if
\delta<1
. Summarizing, we have that under Assumption
6
, for sufficiently large
n
, there exists some positive constant
0<K<\infty
such that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[n^{\delta}L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]

\displaystyle\leq(n-1)^{\delta}L_{n-1}+\underbrace{O\left(n^{-(2-\delta)}\right)}_{C_{n}}-\underbrace{n^{-1}K\,(n-1)^{\delta}L_{n-1}}_{D_{n}}

where
B_{n}=0
and
n^{\delta}L_{n}
is an almost supermartingale, as
C_{n},D_{n}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}C_{i}<\infty
a.s. As a result, Theorem
A4
gives us
n^{\delta}L_{n}\to X_{\infty}
a.s. under Assumption
6
. Finally, we have that
X_{\infty}=0
a.s. which follows from
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}i^{-(1-\delta)}L_{i}<\infty
a.s.

B.9
Theorem
6

We start again have from (
A14
)

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[O(n^{-1})+2O(n^{-1/2})\,d_{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{n},Q^{*})+d_{2}^{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{n},Q^{*})\right].

From Theorem
5
, for each
0<\delta<1
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty},Q^{*}\right)\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[O(n^{-1})+O(n^{-(\delta+1)/2})+O(n^{-\delta})\right]\quad\text{a.s.}[P^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}O(n^{-\delta})\quad\text{a.s.}[P^{*}]

The above means that for all
\varepsilon>0
, for any
0<\delta<1
, there exists some constant
B_{\delta}<\infty
such that we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}(Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger},Q^{*})\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n}\right)\leq\frac{B_{\delta}n^{-\delta}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\quad\text{a.s.}[P^{*}]

for sufficiently large
n
.
Now choose an arbitrary
0<\delta<1
, and also choose
\delta<\delta^{\prime}<1
with corresponding
B_{\delta^{\prime}}
. If we plug-in
\varepsilon_{n}=Kn^{-\delta/2}
as in Theorem
6
for an arbitrary finite positive constant
K
, then we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}(Q^{\dagger}_{n\infty}:d_{2}(Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger},Q^{*})\geq K\varepsilon_{n}\mid Y_{1:n})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B_{\delta^{\prime}}n^{-\delta^{\prime}}}{\varepsilon_{n}^{2}}\quad\text{a.s.}[P^{*}]

\displaystyle=\frac{B_{\delta^{\prime}}}{K^{2}}n^{-\left(\delta^{\prime}-\delta\right)}\quad\text{a.s.}[P^{*}]

for sufficiently large
n
. Since
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
can always be chosen, we have that the above goes to 0 with
n
for any
K>0
.

B.10
Proposition
5

We showed in the proof of Theorem
4
that
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, so we can apply Proposition
1
or Theorem
1
. The probability space can be constructed in the same way as Theorem
4
.

Fix
n\geq 1
and define the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{N}\}_{N\geq n}
with
\mathcal{F}_{N}=\sigma(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n},V_{n+1},\ldots,V_{N})
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
. We will write
\mathbb{E}[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}]
as
\mathbb{E}[\cdot\mid Y_{1:n}]
to make it clear that it is conditioned on ‘real’ data.
To begin, we highlight the very useful property that

\displaystyle\mu_{nN}:=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{nN}^{\dagger}(u)\,du=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{nN}(u)\,du

which follows directly from Lemma
A1
with
h(x)=x
. The above also holds for
N=\infty
, where the existence of
Q_{n\infty}
is guaranteed by Proposition
1
.

This is particularly convenient as we do not need to consider the rearrangement procedure to study the distribution of
\mu_{nN}
.
Note that this property is not unique to the mean functional. Another subtle but important point is that
\{\mu_{nN}\}_{N\geq n}
is a martingale, even if
Q_{nN}^{\dagger}
is not, due to the above property, which follows from

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{nN}\mid\mathcal{F}_{N-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{nN}(u)\mid\mathcal{F}_{N-1}\right]\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{n,N-1}(u)du

\displaystyle=\mu_{n,N-1}.

This arises from the linearity of the mean, which
is
unique to the mean and does not apply for other functionals. We can then directly show that
\mu_{N}
is bounded in
L^{2}
, as
E[X]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]
gives

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{nN}^{2}\mid Y_{1:n}\right]\leq\sup_{N\geq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{nN}\|^{2}_{2}\mid Y_{1:n}\right]<\infty,

where the boundedness was shown in Proposition
1
. As a result,
\mu_{nN}
is a martingale bounded in
L^{2}
, so there exists a finite
\tilde{\mu}_{n\infty}
such that
\mu_{nN}\to\tilde{\mu}_{n\infty}
a.s. and
E[\tilde{\mu}_{n\infty}\mid Y_{1:n}]=\mu_{n}
.
Finally, we have

\displaystyle(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{nN})^{2}\leq d_{2}^{2}(Q_{n\infty},Q_{nN})\to 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.}

from Proposition
1
, so
\tilde{\mu}_{n\infty}=\mu_{n\infty}
a.s.
We thus have the first part of Proposition
5
, that is
E[\mu_{n\infty}\mid Y_{1:n}]=\mu_{n}
a.s. for each
n
.

For the posterior variance, we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n})^{2}\mid Y_{1:n}]=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]

where
Z_{i}=\int_{0}^{1}(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i}))\,du
.
As
Z_{i}
is independent of
Z_{j}
for
i\neq j
and are zero-mean, the cross-terms are zero, so we just have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n})^{2}\mid Y_{1:n}]=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}].

We can then show from Lemma
A7
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u,v)\,du\,dv-\frac{1}{4}\cdot

As
C_{\rho}(u,v)\leq 1
and
\lim_{\rho\to 1}C_{\rho^{2}}(u,v)=\min(u,v)
, dominated convergence gives

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\min(u,v)\,du\,dv-\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}

Scaling by
{n}
gives us

\displaystyle{n}\,\mathbb{E}[(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n})^{2}\mid Y_{1:n}]

\displaystyle=a^{2}\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{{n}}{(i+1)^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}].

Now consider

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{{n}}{(i+1)^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]-\frac{1}{12}\right|\leq\left|\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{{n}}{(i+1)^{2}}\,\left(\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]-\frac{1}{12}\right)+\frac{1}{12}\left(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{{n}}{(i+1)^{2}}-1\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{{n}}{(i+1)^{2}}\,\left|\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]-\frac{1}{12}\right|+\frac{1}{12}\left|\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{{n}}{(i+1)^{2}}-1\right|.

For each
\varepsilon>0
, for sufficiently large
n
, we have
\sup_{i\geq n+1}\left|\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]-\frac{1}{12}\right|<\varepsilon
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\left|n\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(i+1)^{2}}-1\right|\to 0,

which follows as
(n+2)^{-1}\leq\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}(i+1)^{-2}\leq(n+1)^{-1}
from the integral test.
We thus have

\displaystyle{n}\,\mathbb{E}[(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n})^{2}\mid Y_{1:n}]\to\frac{a^{2}}{12}\quad\textnormal{a.s.}[P^{*}]

Note that the result does not depend on the convergence of
\mu_{n}
, as this will always act as the center of the posterior.

B.11
Theorem
7

We begin with showing the inequality. Once again, we have

\displaystyle S_{n}(u)=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i}))

where
V_{i}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{U}(0,1)
and the existence of the random function
S_{n}
with realizations in
H^{1}((0,1))
is guaranteed by Theorem
1
as
\alpha_{i}
and
\rho_{i}
satisfy Assumptions
3
and
4
respectively. It is clear that this function has mean 0. To begin, we have the following lemma.

Lemma A11
.

The covariance function
k_{n}(u,u^{\prime}):=\mathbb{E}[S_{n}(u)\,S_{n}(u^{\prime})]
takes the form

\displaystyle k_{n}(u,u^{\prime})=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,[C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u,u^{\prime})-uu^{\prime}].

Proof.

A direct calculation gives

\displaystyle k_{n}(u,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})\right)\left(u^{\prime}-H_{\rho_{j}}(u^{\prime},V_{j})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\left[C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u,u^{\prime})-uu^{\prime}\right].

The cross-terms are zero, so we can invoke Lemma
A7
which gives the last line.
∎

To get the inequalities, we note that
\rho_{i}^{2}\to 1
and
\rho_{i}^{2}\leq\rho_{j}^{2}
for
i\leq j
, so we can
apply the ordering property of the bivariate Gaussian copula from Section
A.5
, where the ordering holds uniformly over
u,u^{\prime}\in(0,1)
. Note that
\lim_{\rho\to 1}C_{\rho^{2}}(u,u^{\prime})=\min\{u,u^{\prime}\}
, so the squeeze theorem gives the convergence to the Brownian bridge covariance function as
\rho_{n}s\to 1
for both
k_{\rho_{n}}
and
r_{n}^{-1}k_{n}
.

We now turn our focus to the Gaussian process
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}\sim\mathcal{GP}(0,C_{\rho^{2}}(u,u^{\prime})-uu^{\prime})
for some
\rho\in(0,1)
as in the approximate sampling scheme for the QMP. We will show that sample paths of
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}
are in
H^{1}((0,1))
using
Scheuerer [2010, Theorem 1]
. This depends on properties of the partial derivatives of the kernel function, which exists and is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u\partial v}[C_{\rho}(u,v)-uv]=c_{\rho}(u,v)-1.

We then have the following lemma.

Lemma A12
.

The bivariate copula density
c_{\rho}(u,v)
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}[c_{\rho}(u,u)-1]\,du<\infty.

Proof.

We have the following from a change of variables
u\to z_{u}=\Phi^{-1}(u)
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}[c_{\rho}(u,u)-1]\,du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(\frac{2\rho z_{u}^{2}-2\rho^{2}z_{u}^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right)\phi(z_{u})\,dz_{u}-1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1-\rho^{2})}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(\frac{z_{u}^{2}\left(2\rho-1-\rho^{2}\right)}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right)\,dz_{u}-1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1-\rho^{2})}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}(1-\rho)}{2(1+\rho)}\right)\,dz_{u}-1<\infty

where the finiteness follows as
(1-\rho)/(1+\rho)>0
.
∎

Continuity of
c_{\rho}(u,u)
for all
u\in(0,1)
and the above lemma means that the covariance function
k_{\rho}
satisfies the conditions of
Scheuerer [2010, Theorem 1]
, so sample paths of
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}
are in
H^{1}((0,1))=W^{1,2}((0,1))
a.s.

Finally, we show the weak convergence to the Brownian motion. Marginal convergence is quite obvious as the covariance function approaches
\min\{u,u^{\prime}\}-uu^{\prime}
with
n\to\infty
. However, showing tightness of the sequence of GPs requires a bit more work.

Lemma A13
.

The sequence
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}((0,1))
.

Proof.

We begin by computing the standard deviation semimetric of
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)^{2}\right]-2\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)\,\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)\right]

\displaystyle=C_{\rho_{n}^{2}}(u,u)-u^{2}+C_{\rho_{n}^{2}}(v,v)-{v}^{2}-2\left(C_{\rho_{n}^{2}}(u,v)-uv\right)

From
Meyer [2013]
, we have the following property:

\displaystyle\min\{u,v\}-C_{\rho}(u,v)=\int_{\rho}^{1}\phi_{2}\left(z_{u},z_{v};\rho\right)\,dr

where

\displaystyle\phi_{2}(z_{u},z_{v})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2}-2\rho z_{u}z_{v}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right).

For
\mathbb{G}\sim\mathcal{GP}(0,\min\{u,u^{\prime}\}-uu^{\prime})
as the Brownian bridge, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}(u)-\mathbb{G}(v)\right)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\rho_{n}^{2}}^{1}\frac{g_{r}(z_{u},z_{v})}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\,dr

where

\displaystyle g_{r}(z_{u},z_{v})=\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}}{1+r}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{z_{v}^{2}}{1+r}\right)-2\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2}-2rz_{u}z_{v}}{2(1-r^{2})}\right).

Completing the square gives

\displaystyle g_{r}(z_{u},z_{v})

\displaystyle=\left[\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}}{2(1+r)}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{z_{v}^{2}}{2(1+r)}\right)\right]^{2}+2\left[\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2}}{2(1+r)}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2}-2rz_{u}z_{v}}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)\right]

The second term can be written as

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{(z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2})(1-r)}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2}-2rz_{u}z_{v}}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{(z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2})(1-r)}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)\left[1-\exp\left(-\frac{r(z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2}-2z_{u}z_{v})}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{(z_{u}^{2}+z_{v}^{2})(1-r)}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)\left[1-\exp\left(-\frac{r(z_{u}-z_{v})^{2}}{2(1-r^{2})}\right)\right]

Since
(z_{u}-z_{v})^{2}\geq 0
, we have that the above is non-negative, so
g_{r}(z_{u},z_{v})\geq 0
for all
z_{u},z_{v}\in\mathbb{R}
. This thus gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)\right)^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}(u)-\mathbb{G}(v)\right)^{2}\right]

(A15)

for all
n
.
Consider the semimetric space
(\mathcal{F}=(0,1),d)
where
d(u,v)=|u-v|^{1/2}
.
It is clear that
\mathcal{F}
is totally bounded under this semimetric.
Let
d_{n}
and
d_{\infty}
denote the standard deviation semimetrics of
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
and
\mathbb{G}
respectively, which are

\displaystyle d_{n}^{2}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle d_{\infty}^{2}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{G}(u)-\mathbb{G}(v)\right)^{2}\right]=|u-v|(1-|u-v|).

As
|u-v|<1
, we have
d_{\infty}(u,v)\leq d(u,v)
, which combined with (
A15
) gives

\displaystyle d_{n}(u,v)\leq d_{\infty}(u,v)\leq d(u,v)

for all
u,v\in(0,1)
and
n
.

Let
D(\varepsilon,d)
denote the packing number of the space
(\mathcal{F},d)
.

Van der Vaart and Wellner [2023, Corollary 2.2.9]
states that for
X
as a separable Gaussian process with
d_{X}
as its standard deviation semimetric, we have for every
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{d_{X}(u,v)\leq\delta}|X(u)-X(v)|\right]\leq K\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log D(\varepsilon,d_{X})}\,d\varepsilon

for a universal constant
K
.

In particular, as we have
d_{n}(u,v)\leq d(u,v)
, this implies
that
\{u,v:d(u,v)\leq\delta\}\subseteq\{u,v:d_{n}(u,v)\leq\delta\}
, which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{d(u,v)\leq\delta}|\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)|\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{d_{n}(u,v)\leq\delta}|\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)|\right]\leq K\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log D(\varepsilon,\rho_{n})}\,d\varepsilon.

Furthermore, since
d_{n}(u,v)\leq d(u,v)
where
d(u,v)=|u-v|^{1/2}
, the packing numbers similarly satisfy

\displaystyle D(\varepsilon,d_{n})\leq D(\varepsilon,d).

Under the semimetric
d
, the packing number for any
\varepsilon>0
satisfies

\displaystyle D(\varepsilon,d)\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}

for a universal constant
C
. We thus have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log D(\varepsilon,\rho)}\,d\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log C+2\log\varepsilon^{-1}}\,d\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{0}^{\delta}(\log C+2\log\varepsilon^{-1})\,d\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\delta\left[\log C+2(1+\log\delta^{-1})\right]}

where the second line follows from Jensen’s inequality. The above can be made arbitrarily small by decreasing
\delta
. Finally, Markov’s inequality gives

\displaystyle\limsup_{n}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{d(u,v)\leq\delta}|\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\limsup_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{d(u,v)\leq\delta}|\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)-\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(v)|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{\delta\left[\log C+2(1+\log\delta^{-1})\right]}.

We can make the right hand side less than any
\eta>0
by sufficiently decreasing
\delta
, so
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
is asymptotically uniformly
d
-equicontinuous in probability.

For each
u\in(0,1)
, uniform tightness of the sequence
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\rho_{n}^{2}}(u,u)-u^{2})
can be verified with

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\,\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)|>M\right)\leq\sup_{n}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)^{2}\right]}{M^{2}}\leq\frac{u(1-u)}{M^{2}}\cdot

For any
\varepsilon>0
, we can choose
M^{2}>u(1-u)/\varepsilon
which gives
\sup_{n}\,\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}(u)|>M\right)<\varepsilon
. From
Van der Vaart and Wellner [2023, Theorem 1.5.7]
, the sequence
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}((0,1))
.
∎

Finally, the marginals of
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
are simply zero-mean Gaussian vectors with covariance matrix with entries
C_{\rho_{n}^{2}}(u_{i},u_{j})-u_{i}u_{j}
. Each entry converges pointwise from below to
\min\{u_{i},u_{j}\}-u_{i}u_{j}
as
\rho_{n}\to 1
, so from Lévy’s continuity theorem, the marginals converge to a zero-mean Gaussian vector with the appropriate covariance matrix. From
Van der Vaart and Wellner [2023, Theorem 1.5.4]
,
\mathbb{G}_{\rho_{n}}
converges weakly to
\mathbb{G}
in
\ell^{\infty}((0,1))
.

B.12
Theorem
8

The additional required assumptions of Theorem
8
are as follows, which is analogous to Assumptions
1
and
2
from the unconditional version.

Assumption A1

(Bounded in
L^{2}
)
.

For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
,
\beta_{nj}
satisfies
\|\beta_{nj}\|_{2}<\infty
.

Assumption A2

(Weak derivatives bounded in
L^{2}
)
.

For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
,
\beta_{nj}
is weakly differentiable with weak derivative
\beta^{\prime}_{nj}
which satisfies
\|\beta^{\prime}_{nj}\|_{2}<\infty
, so
\|\beta_{nj}\|_{1,2}<\infty
.

The proof is an extension of Theorem
1
, with the additional complication of random covariates
X_{n+1:\infty}
arising from the Bayesian bootstrap. Let
B^{\prime}=H^{1}((0,1))
, and consider a single component
j\in\{1\ldots,p\}
. We then have the update

\displaystyle\beta_{N+1,j}(u)=\beta_{Nj}(u)+\alpha_{N+1}\left[u-H_{\rho_{N+1}}(u,V_{N+1})\right]X_{N+1,j}

for each
u\in(0,1)
and
N\geq n
, where
V_{N+1}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{U}(0,1)
and
X_{N+1}\mid X_{1:N}\sim\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}}
. Our filtration now consists of
\mathcal{F}_{N}=\sigma\left(X_{n+1},V_{n+1},\ldots,X_{N},V_{N}\right)

for
N\geq n+1
, with
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
again.

The above is again a pointwise martingale, as we have
\int_{0}^{1}H_{\rho}(u,v)\,dv=u
which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{N+1,j}(u)\mid\mathcal{F}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{N+1,j}(u)\mid\mathcal{F}_{N},X_{N+1}\right]\mid\mathcal{F}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{Nj}(u)\mid\mathcal{F}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle=\beta_{Nj}(u).

Note that the (conditional) distribution of
X_{N+1}
does not affect the martingale. The argument using continuous bounded functionals in Lemma
A9
can be repeated here to show that

\{\beta_{Nj}\}_{N\geq n+1}
is a
B^{\prime}
-valued martingale if
\beta_{Nj}\in B^{\prime}
. We can then upper bound the
L^{2}(B^{\prime})
norm as in Theorem
1
with

\displaystyle\|\beta_{Nj}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\prime})}\leq\|\beta_{nj}\|_{1,2}+\sup_{k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}X_{kj}^{2}\sum_{i=n+1}^{N}\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2}}{1-\rho_{i}^{2}},

where we have used the fact that
X_{n+1:N}
will be repeats of
X_{1:n}
and

\sup_{k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}X_{kj}^{2}
is finite as we only have finitely many (i.e.
n
) covariate observations.
Under Assumptions
3
,
4
,
A1
and
A2
, we thus have
\beta_{Nj}\in B^{\prime}
for each
N
and
\sup_{N\geq n}\|\beta_{Nj}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\prime})}<\infty
, so we can apply Theorem
A1
.

We can repeat the above for all components
j\in(1,\ldots,p)
, and as
p
is finite, the union of the null sets on which convergence does not occur for each component has measure 0, so the vector
\beta_{N}
converges to
\beta_{\infty}
component-wise a.s.

We now describe the space of vector functions
\beta_{N}
and
\beta_{\infty}
, which we will need for a later proof. Consider the finite product of Banach spaces

\displaystyle H^{1}((0,1))^{p}:=H^{1}((0,1))\times\ldots\times H^{1}((0,1)).

For a vector
f\in H^{1}((0,1))^{p}
, we define the norm of this Banach space as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{1,2,p}:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|f_{j}\|_{1,2}.

It is clear that
\beta_{N},\beta_{\infty}\in H^{1}((0,1))^{p}
, and as each component converges a.s., we have

\displaystyle\|\beta_{N}-\beta_{\infty}\|_{1,2,p}\to 0\quad\text{a.s.}

B.13
Proposition
6

For an arbitrary
x\in\mathcal{X}
, consider the mapping
h_{x}:H^{1}((0,1))^{p}\to H^{1}((0,1))
defined by

\displaystyle h_{x}(\beta)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{j}x_{j}

where
x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
is the
j
-th component of
x
. This mapping can be shown to be continuous as follows. Consider a sequence
\beta_{N}\to\beta_{\infty}
in
H^{1}((0,1))^{p}
, then we have

\displaystyle\|h_{x}(\beta_{N})-h_{x}(\beta_{\infty})\|_{1,2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|(\beta_{Nj}-\beta_{\infty j})\,x_{j}\|_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}|x_{j}|\,\|\beta_{Nj}-\beta_{\infty j}\,\|_{1,2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{j}|x_{j}|\,\|\beta_{N}-\beta_{\infty}\|_{1,2,p}\to 0,

where we have applied the triangle inequality in the first step. For
Q_{N}(\cdot\mid x):=h_{x}(\beta_{N})
and
Q_{\infty}(\cdot\mid x):=h_{x}(\beta_{\infty})
from Theorem
8
, the continuous mapping theorem then gives

\displaystyle d_{1,2}\left(Q_{N}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{\infty}(\cdot\mid x)\right)\to 0\quad\text{a.s.}

From Theorem
A2
, we have that
Q_{N}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x)
and
Q_{\infty}(\cdot\mid x)
are in
H^{1}((0,1))
. We can thus apply Proposition
A3
to give

\displaystyle d_{2}(Q^{\dagger}_{N}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{\infty}(\cdot\mid x))\to 0\quad\text{a.s.}

B.14
Proposition
7

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we can once again apply Lemma
A1
with
h(x)=x
, which gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}Q^{\dagger}_{\infty}(u\mid x)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{\infty}(u\mid x)\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\beta_{\infty}(u)^{T}x\,du\quad\textnormal{a.s.}

Linearity of expectation then gives
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}\left[Y\mid x\right]=\left[\int_{0}^{1}\beta_{\infty}(u)\,du\right]^{T}x
, which is a (random) linear function in
x
a.s.

B.15
Theorem
9

We follow the same strategy as the proof for Theorem
2
. We will use Theorem
A3
to verify asymptotic tightness, and then we establish marginal convergence. To reduce clutter, we will suppress the conditioning on the weights in the notation. Define

C_{X}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}\end{subarray}}|X_{ij}|.

Theorem A6
.

The sequence of functions
\sqrt{N}S_{N}
is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
with probability 1.

Proof.

We verify the assumptions in Theorem
A3
. Our semimetric space is
(\mathcal{F}=(0,1)\times\{1,\ldots,p\},d)
, where
d((u_{1},j_{1}),(u_{2},j_{2}))=|u_{1}-u_{2}|^{1/2}+\mathbbm{1}(j_{1}\neq j_{2})
. We have used the discrete metric on
\{1,\ldots,p\}
and then specified the sum of the two semimetrics to define the semimetric product space. Clearly, this semimetric space is totally bounded.

First define
Z_{Ni}(u,j)=\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}(H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})-u)X_{ij}
for all
N
and
i\geq N
. Note that this definition differs in nature to the non-regression case (where the
-u
term can be omitted) because the randomness in the covariates must be accounted for. A trivial envelope function for
Z_{Ni}
is
F_{Ni}=2\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}C_{X}
. We need to verify the Lindeberg condition

\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}F_{Ni}\mathbbm{1}\{F_{Ni}>\eta\}\rightarrow 0

for every
\eta>0
. Since
F_{Ni}<2cC_{X}/\sqrt{N}
, we will have
F_{Ni}<\eta
for all sufficiently large
N
. Thus, the Lindeberg condition holds.

Next we need

\sup_{d((u_{1},j_{1}),(u_{2},j_{2}))<\delta_{N}}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1})-Z_{Ni}(u_{2})\right\}^{2}\rightarrow 0

for every
\delta_{N}\downarrow 0
. For all sufficiently large
N
, we must have
\delta_{N}<1
, in which case

d((u_{1},j_{1}),(u_{2},j_{2}))<\delta_{N}\implies j_{1}=j_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|u_{1}-u_{2}|^{1/2}<\delta_{N}.

Let
u_{1}>u_{2}
with
|u_{1}-u_{2}|^{1/2}<\delta_{N}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1},j)-Z_{Ni}(u_{2},j)\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left\{\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}X_{ij}H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{1},V_{i})-\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}X_{ij}H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{2},V_{i})\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\mathbb{E}\left\{\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}X_{ij}u_{1}-\sqrt{N}\alpha_{i}X_{ij}u_{2}\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2N\alpha_{i}^{2}C_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\{H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{1},V_{i})-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{2},V_{i})\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle+2N\alpha_{i}^{2}C_{X}^{2}\delta_{N}^{2}

Note that
H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})
is non-decreasing in
u
with
H_{\rho_{i}}(0,V_{i})=0
and
H_{\rho_{i}}(1,V_{i})=1
. So

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{1},V_{i})-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{2},V_{i})\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left\{H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{1},V_{i})-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{2},V_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle=(u_{1}-u_{2})

\displaystyle<\delta_{N}^{2}.

Thus, for all sufficiently large
N
such that
\delta_{N}<1
, we have

\sup_{d((u_{1},j_{1}),(u_{2},j_{2}))<\delta_{N}}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\{Z_{Ni}(u_{1},j_{1})-Z_{Ni}(u_{2},j_{2})\right\}^{2}<4N\delta_{N}^{2}C_{X}^{2}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}.

Since
\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq c^{2}
, the right-hand side of the above display tends to zero for any
\delta_{N}\downarrow 0
.

Finally, we need to verify the bracketing entropy integral condition. Fix
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
for the time being. For all sufficiently large
n
, we will have
N\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq 2c^{2}
. Given
\varepsilon>0
, choose a partition
0=u_{0}<u_{1}<\ldots<u_{M}=1
such that
u_{k}-u_{k-1}<\varepsilon^{2}/(8C_{X}^{2}c^{2})
for every
k
. The number of points in the partition can be chosen to be smaller than a constant times
1/\varepsilon^{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{u_{k-1}\leq s,t<u_{k}}|Z_{Ni}(s,j)-Z_{Ni}(t,j)|^{2}

\displaystyle<2N\alpha^{2}_{i}C_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\{H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{k},V_{i})-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{k-1},V_{i})\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle+2N\alpha^{2}_{i}C_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\sup_{u_{k-1}\leq s,t<u_{k}}|s-t|^{2}

\displaystyle<2N\alpha^{2}_{i}C_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\{H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{k},V_{i})-H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{k-1},V_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle+2N\alpha^{2}_{i}C_{X}^{2}(u_{k}-u_{k-1})

\displaystyle<\frac{N\alpha_{i}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2c^{2}}.

We deduce that for all sufficiently large
N
,

\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\sup_{u_{j-1}\leq s,t<u_{j}}|Z_{Ni}(s)-Z_{Ni}(t)|^{2}<\varepsilon^{2}.

In other words,
\mathfrak{N}_{[]}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{2,n})\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, which verifies the entropy condition.

Thus, Theorem
A3
implies that

-\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}Z_{Ni}(u,j)=\sqrt{N}\sum_{i=N}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i}))X_{ij}

is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

Proposition A5
.

If
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{d})\in(0,1)^{d}
and
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in\{1,\ldots,p\}^{d}
, then

\sqrt{N}[S_{N}(u_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,S_{N}(u_{d},j_{d})]^{T}\mid w_{1:n}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,a^{2}\Sigma)

as
N\rightarrow\infty
, where
\Sigma_{l,m}=\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}X_{kj_{l}}X_{kj_{m}}\right](\min\{u_{l},u_{m}\}-u_{l}u_{m})
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary vector
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we study the convergence of

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\,t_{l}\,S_{N}(u_{l},j_{l}).

Consider

\displaystyle Z_{Ni}[\mathbf{t}]=\sqrt{N}\,\alpha_{i}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\,t_{l}\,(H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{l},V_{i})-u_{l})X_{ij}

which has expectation
0
. We have that

\displaystyle|Z_{Ni}[\mathbf{t}]|\leq 2aC_{X}\frac{\sqrt{N}}{i}\sum_{l=1}^{d}|t_{j}|,

so for every
\varepsilon>0
,
|Z_{Ni}[\mathbf{t}]|<\varepsilon
eventually, which verifies the Lindeberg condition for the Lindeberg-Feller CLT. For the limiting variance, we first have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}[\mathbf{t}]^{2}]

\displaystyle=a^{2}\frac{N}{i^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}t_{l}t_{m}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}X_{kj_{l}}X_{kj_{m}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\left(\mathbb{E}\left[H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{j},V_{i})\,H_{\rho_{i}}(u_{k},V_{i})\right]-u_{l}u_{m}\right)

If we take
N/m_{N}=o(1)
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{i=N}^{N+m_{N}}\mathbb{E}[Z_{Ni}[\mathbf{t}^{2}]\to\sigma^{2}=a^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}t_{l}t_{m}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}X_{kj_{l}}X_{kj_{m}}\right]\left(\min\{u_{l},u_{m}\}-u_{l}u_{m}\right)

via similar computations to the non-regression case. So we can apply the Lindeberg-Feller CLT to the sequence of sums up to
m_{N}
. We check that the tail sums from
m_{N}+1
onwards are asymptotically negligible:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}\text{Var}[Z_{Ni}[\mathbf{t}]]

\displaystyle=N\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}t_{l}t_{m}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}X_{kj_{l}}X_{kj_{m}}\right]\left(C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u_{l},u_{m})-u_{l}u_{m}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{l=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}t_{l}t_{m}C_{X}^{2}\right|N\sum_{i=m_{N}+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\to 0.

Finally, the Cramér-Wold device gives us

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}[S_{N}(u_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,S_{N}(u_{d},j_{d})]^{T}\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},a^{2}\Sigma)

where
\Sigma_{l,m}=\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}X_{kj_{l}}X_{kj_{m}}\right](\min\{u_{l},u_{m}\}-u_{l}u_{m})
.
∎

B.16
Proposition
8

We require the following assumption on the covariance matrix of the covariates.

Assumption A3

(Covariance matrix of covariates)
.

The covariance matrix of the covariate distribution
\Sigma_{x}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}xx^{T}\,dP^{*}(x)
is positive definite, and all elements are finite.

We follow a similar approach to Proposition
5
. Let
Y_{i},X_{i}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}P^{*}(y,x)
, and
\beta_{n}
is computed by Algorithm
6
.
Following the same argument as in Theorem
4
for each component of
\beta_{n}
, one can see that
\beta_{n}\in H^{1}((0,1))^{p}
for each
n
, thus satisfying Assumptions
A1
and
A2
. This allows us to apply Theorem
8
giving the existence of
\beta_{n\infty}
. To construct the probability space, we can again consider a single sequence
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots
of uniform r.v.s.
For each
n
, consider the Bayesian bootstrap starting with
X_{1:n}
, i.e. we have

\displaystyle w_{1:n}

\displaystyle\sim\text{Dir}(1,\ldots,1),\quad X_{nN}\mid w_{1:n},X_{1:n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{X_{i}}

for
N\geq n+1
, where the additional subscript
n
on
X
indicates how many ‘real’ observations we start predictive resampling from.
We can then define for each
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\beta_{n\infty}(u)=\beta_{n}(u)+\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\left(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})\right)\,X_{ni}.

We now consider the posterior distribution of the mean functional
\bar{\beta}_{n\infty}
where

\displaystyle\bar{\beta}_{n\infty},=\int_{0}^{1}\beta_{n\infty}(u)\,du.

Fubini’s theorem gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bar{\beta}_{n\infty}\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}]=\bar{\beta}_{n}.

The posterior covariance matrix is thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[b_{n}b_{n}^{T}\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}Z_{i}\,X_{ni}\right)\left(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}Z_{i}\,X_{ni}\right)^{T}\Big|Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]

where we write
b_{n}=\bar{\beta}_{n\infty}-\bar{\beta}_{n}
and
Z_{i}=\int_{0}^{1}\left(u-H_{\rho_{i}}(u,V_{i})\right)\,du
for shorthand.
Let us first condition on
w_{1:n}
, which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[b_{n}b_{n}^{T}\mid w_{1:n},Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}]=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}Z_{j}\,X_{ni}X_{nj}^{T}\mid w_{1:n},Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right].

As
Z_{i}
and
Z_{j}
are independent for
i\neq j
, and the covariates are independent from the uniform r.v.s, the cross-terms are all 0, so the above simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]\,\mathbb{E}[X_{ni}X_{ni}^{T}\mid w_{1:n},Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}].

As before, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\,C_{\rho_{i}^{2}}(u,v)\,du\,dv-\frac{1}{4}

and now we have the additional term

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{ni}X_{ni}^{T}\mid w_{1:n},X_{1:n}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}.

This gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[b_{n}b_{n}^{T}\mid w_{1:n},Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}]=\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}].

The tower property then gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[b_{n}b_{n}^{T}\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}]=\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}].

We can then scale this by
{n}
and take the limit, giving us

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{n}\,\mathbb{E}[b_{n}b_{n}^{T}\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}\right]\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[n\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}}{12}\Sigma_{x}\quad P^{*}\text{-a.s.}

Appendix C
Additional results

C.1
Asymptotic distribution of mean functional

For the mean functional, we only computed its posterior mean and asymptotic variance in Proposition
5
. We can actually extend this in the unconditional case and quantify the asymptotic distribution of

\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n}
due to it being a sum of independent terms. This could potentially lead the way to future Bernstein-von Mises for functionals of the QMP. However, as mentioned in the main paper, quantifying the distribution of
\mu_{n}
is more challenging.

Proposition A6
.

Let
\mu_{n}=\int Q^{\dagger}_{n}(u)\,du
for
\{Q^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
from Algorithm
3
, and
\mu_{n\infty}=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(u)\,du
where
Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}
arises from Algorithm
4
starting from
Q^{\dagger}_{n}
. Under Assumptions
3
and
4
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n}\right)\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,a^{2}/12)

Proof.

We will extend the proof of Proposition
5
by applying Theorem
2
, although we highlight that one can also prove the above using the standard Lindeberg-Feller CLT for scalar r.v.s.
In order to apply Theorem
2
, we will leverage the specific construction for
Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}
as in the proof of Theorem
4
. Let us define

\displaystyle S_{N}(u)=\sum_{i=N+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\left[u-H_{\rho_{i}}\left(u,V_{i}\right)\right]

for
n\geq 1
, where
V_{i}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{U}(0,1)
. We then define

\displaystyle Q_{n\infty}=Q_{n}^{\dagger}+S_{n},

for
n\geq 1
. For each
n
,
Q_{n\infty}
has the same distribution as that induced by Algorithm
4
, but is not independent across
n
. However, this is inconsequential as we are studying a weak limit. This gives a corresponding sequence of random means:

\displaystyle\mu_{n\infty}=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{n\infty}(u)\,du.

We then clearly have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{n}\,S_{n}(u)\,du.

Since the weak limit of
\sqrt{N}S_{N}
is independent of the initial estimate
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
, we can apply Theorem
2
directly. This gives
\sqrt{n}S_{n}\to\mathbb{G}_{a}
weakly in
\ell^{\infty}((0,1))
, where
\mathbb{G}_{a}
is a zero-mean GP with covariance function

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{G}_{a}(u)\,\mathbb{G}_{a}(u^{\prime})\right]=a^{2}(\min\{u,u^{\prime}\}-uu^{\prime})
.

Consider now the integral operator
h:\ell^{\infty}((0,1))\to\mathbb{R}
where
h(f)=\int_{0}^{1}\,f(u)\,du
. It is not too hard to see that this is a continuous function, as for any sequence
f_{n}\to f_{\infty}
for
f_{n},f_{\infty}\in\ell^{\infty}((0,1))
, we have

\displaystyle|h(f_{n})-h(f_{\infty})|\leq\int_{0}^{1}|f(u)-f_{\infty}(u)|\,du\leq\|f_{n}-f_{\infty}\|_{\infty}\to 0.

The continuous mapping theorem then gives us

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\mu_{n\infty}-\mu_{n})\overset{d}{\to}h(\mathbb{G}_{a}).

We now show that
h(\mathbb{G}_{a})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,a^{2}/12)
. It is clear that
h(\mathbb{G}_{a})
has mean zero, and its variance is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[h(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{G}_{a}(u)\,\mathbb{G}_{a}(u^{\prime})\,du\,du^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{G}_{a}(u)\,\mathbb{G}_{a}(u^{\prime})\right]\,du\,du^{\prime}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\left[\min\{u,u^{\prime}\}-uu^{\prime}\right]du\,du^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}}{12}\cdot

The normality of
h(\mathbb{G}_{a})
then follows from an approximating Riemann sum argument (e.g.
[
Hassler, 2016
, Chapter 8.3]
) as sample paths of the Brownian motion are continuous a.s.
∎

C.2
Frequentist consistency for quantile regression

In this section, we outline a posterior consistency result for the QMP for quantile regression with
\rho=1
. This setting lends itself more easily to a consistent initial estimate. Consider the QMP with the updates

\displaystyle\beta_{N+1}(u)

\displaystyle=\beta_{N}(u)+\alpha_{N+1}\left[u-G_{N+1}(\beta_{N},Y_{N+1},X_{N+1})\right]\,X_{N+1}

(A16)

\displaystyle G_{N}(\beta,Y,X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}\left(Y\leq\beta(u)^{T}X\right)\quad&\text{for }N\leq n\\
\mathbbm{1}\left(Y\leq Q^{\dagger}(u\mid X)\right)\quad&\text{for }N\geq n+1\end{cases}

(A17)

where
Q^{\dagger}(u\mid X)
is the increasing rearrangement of
\beta(u)^{T}X
.
The difference between the update for the initial estimate and predictive resampling is subtle but important for both consistency and the martingale.
To derive the latter form, note that

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\to 1}H_{\rho}(u,V_{N+1})=\mathbbm{1}\left(V_{N+1}\leq u\right)=\mathbbm{1}\left(Y_{N+1}\leq Q_{N}^{\dagger}(u\mid X_{N+1})\right)

which is obtained by applying the proper quantile function
Q_{N}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid X_{N+1})
to both sides of the inequality. The martingale under predictive resampling is thus preserved in this case.
For the ‘real data’ update however, we opt to use the standard stochastic approximation estimate of
\beta_{n}
, as it is non-trivial to derive a copula-smoothed version of the above initial estimate (i.e. an equivalent version of (
9
) for
\beta_{n}^{\dagger}(u)
). Consider now the following assumptions on the data generating distribution.

Assumption A4

(Covariate distribution)
.

P^{*}(x)
has compact support and the covariance matrix
\Sigma_{x}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}xx^{T}\,dP^{*}(x)
is positive definite.

Assumption A5

(Linear quantiles)
.

There exists some true function
\beta^{*}(u)
such that the quantile function corresponding to
P^{*}(\cdot\mid x)
takes the form
Q^{*}(u\mid x)=\beta^{*}(u)^{T}x
for all
x
in the support of
P^{*}(x)
.

Assumption A6

(Lipschitz continuity)
.

There exists a finite
L
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}}|\beta^{*}_{j}(u)-\beta^{*}_{j}(u^{\prime})|\leq L|u-u^{\prime}|

where
\beta^{*}_{j}(u)
is the
j
-th component of the vector
\beta^{*}(u)
. Assume that the initial vector function
\beta_{0}
also satisfies the above Lipschitz condition.

We now define the norms to study the conditional quantile function

\displaystyle{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q(\cdot\mid x))=\int_{\mathcal{X}}d^{2}_{2}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q(\cdot\mid x))\,dP^{*}(x)

which is the covariate average
L^{2}
distance between the conditional quantiles, and also

\displaystyle{d}^{2}_{2,p}(\beta^{*},\beta)=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)^{T}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)\,du.

We have a standard result from stochastic approximation arguments:

Proposition A7
.

Under Assumptions
A4
,
A5
and
A6
, we have that

{d}_{2,p}(\beta^{*},\beta_{n})\to 0
a.s.
[P^{*}]

as
n\to\infty
under (
A16
) and (
A17
) with the
N\leq n
form of
G_{N}
.

Proof.

The
L^{2}
distance can be expanded recursively:

\displaystyle d^{2}_{2,p}\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=d^{2}_{2,p}\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n}\right)-2\alpha_{n+1}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)^{T}X_{n+1}\left(u-\mathbbm{1}\left(Y_{n+1}\leq\beta_{n}(u)^{T}X_{n+1}\right)\,\right)\,du

\displaystyle+\alpha_{n+1}^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(u-\mathbbm{1}\left(Y_{n+1}\leq\beta_{n}(u)^{T}X_{n+1}\right)\right)du\,X_{n+1}^{T}X_{n+1}\

Taking the conditional expectation of the above given
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
for
Z_{i}=(Y_{i},X_{i})
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d^{2}_{2,p}\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n+1}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq d^{2}_{2,p}\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n}\right)-2\alpha_{n+1}g(\beta^{*},\beta_{n})+\alpha_{n+1}^{2}V^{2}

where
V^{2}=\mathbb{E}[X^{T}X]
for
X\sim P^{*}(x)
which is finite by Assumption
A4
, and

\displaystyle g(\beta^{*},\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)^{T}x\left(u-P^{*}\left(\beta(u)^{T}x\mid x\right)\right)\,du\,dP^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q(u\mid x)\right)\left(u-P^{*}\left(Q(u\mid x)\mid x\right)\right)\,du\,dP^{*}(x).

For each value of
(u,x)
, we have that

\displaystyle\left(Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q(u\mid x)\right)\left(u-P^{*}\left(Q(u\mid x)\mid x\right)\right)\geq 0.

which follows as
P^{*}(\cdot\mid x)
is monotonic, so we have

\displaystyle\left(Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q(u\mid x)\right)\geq 0\implies\left(u-P^{*}\left(Q(u\mid x)\mid x\right)\right)\geq 0.

We thus have
g(\beta^{*},\beta)\geq 0
and
V^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\alpha_{n+1}^{2}}<\infty
which gives us the almost supermartingale from Theorem
A4
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{2}_{2,p}\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n}\right)\to d_{\infty}\quad\text{a.s.},\quad\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{n}g\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n}\right)<\infty\quad\text{a.s }

We now seek to show
d_{\infty}=0
a.s.
Let
C_{X}
denote the magnitude of the maximum value of
\mathcal{X}
in all dimensions which is finite by Assumption
A4
. Note that we have

\displaystyle\left|Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q^{*}(u^{\prime}\mid x)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta^{*}(u^{\prime})\right)^{T}x\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{X}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left|\beta^{*}_{j}(u)-\beta^{*}_{j}(u^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{X}\,p\,L\,|u-u^{\prime}|

where
L
is the Lipschitz constant from Assumption
A6
.
We thus have
M=C_{X}pL<\infty
such that

\displaystyle|Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q(u\mid x)|\leq M\left|u-P^{*}\left(Q(u\mid x)\mid x\right)\right|

where we have plugged in
u^{\prime}=P^{*}\left(Q(u\mid x)\mid x\right)
, and
M
is chosen uniformly over
x
. Then this gives

\displaystyle g(\beta^{*},\beta)\geq M^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q(u\mid x)\right)^{2}\,\,du\,dP^{*}(x)

With the above, we have

\displaystyle g(\beta^{*},\beta)

\displaystyle\geq M^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)^{T}xx^{T}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)\,du\,dP^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=M^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)^{T}\Sigma_{x}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta(u)\right)\,du

where
\Sigma=\int xx^{T}dP^{*}(x)
and we have used Tonelli’s theorem.
As
\Sigma_{x}
is positive definite by Assumption
A4
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{x^{T}\Sigma_{x}x}{x^{T}x}\geq\lambda_{\text{min}}>0

where
\lambda_{\text{min}}
is the minimum eigenvalue of
\Sigma_{x}
. Therefore, we have that there exists
\varepsilon=\lambda_{\text{min}}M^{-1}>0
such that

\displaystyle g(\beta^{*},\beta_{n})\geq\varepsilon\,d^{2}_{2,p}\left(\beta^{*},\beta_{n}\right).

As
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{n}g(\beta^{*},\beta_{n})<\infty
a.s., this then ensures that
d_{\infty}=0
a.s. by the usual argument.
∎

We can show that consistency of
\beta_{n}
implies consistency of the conditional quantiles.

Corollary A2
.

Under Assumptions
A4
,
A5
and
A6
, we have that

{d}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\to 0
a.s.
[P^{*}]

as
n\to\infty
under (
A16
) and (
A17
) with the
N\leq n
form of
G_{N}
.

Proof.

First, we write

\displaystyle{d}^{2}_{2,x}\left(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(Q^{*}(u\mid x)-Q_{n}(u\mid x)\right)^{2}\,du\,dP^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)^{T}xx^{T}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)\,du\,dP^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)^{T}\Sigma_{x}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)\,du

which looks familiar. Using the other side of the inequality for Rayleigh’s quotient, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)^{T}\Sigma_{x}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)\,du\leq\lambda_{\text{max}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)^{T}\left(\beta^{*}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)\,du

where
\lambda_{\text{max}}
is the maximum eigenvalue of the covariance matrix, which is bounded due to compact support from Assumption
A4
. We then have the desired result as
{d}^{2}_{2,x}\left(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x)\right)\leq\lambda_{\text{max}}\,d_{2,p}^{2}(\beta^{*},\beta_{n})\to 0
a.s.
∎

Posterior consistency can then be showed as follows, where we work directly with
Q^{\dagger}(u\mid x)
instead of
\beta_{\infty}(u)
due to need to take into account the rearrangement operator. The setup is the same as Theorem
4
, where we extend the probability space as before, with the additional ingredients of a vector of weights
w_{1:n}
and random covariates
\{X_{n,n+1},X_{n,n+1},\ldots\}
for each
n
arising from the Bayesian bootstrap.

Theorem A7
.

Under Assumptions
A4
,
A5
and
A6
, the QMP with
\rho=1
as in (
A16
) and (
A17
) is consistent, that is for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pi\left({d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x))\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right)\to 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.}[P^{*}]

Proof.

Once again, Markov’s inequality gives us

\displaystyle\Pi\left({d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x))\geq\varepsilon\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right].

Expanding out the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle E\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]

(A18)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle+2E\left[{d}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]{d}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{n}(\cdot\mid x))

\displaystyle+{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{n}(\cdot\mid x)).

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x)
and
Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x)
has compact range from Assumption
A6
. We can thus apply Proposition
2
to show

{d}^{2}_{2}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x))\leq{d}^{2}_{2}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q(\cdot\mid x))
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, which gives

\displaystyle{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x))\leq{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q(\cdot\mid x)).

For the final term then, we have

\displaystyle{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\leq{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q^{*}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\to 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.}[P^{*}]

For the first term , we also apply the rearrangement inequality to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}^{\dagger}(\cdot\mid x),Q^{\dagger}_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]

which we now bound. First, we look at the inner term

\displaystyle{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left[\left(\beta_{n\infty}(u)-\beta_{n}(u)\right)^{T}x\right]^{2}\,du\,dP^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\,\alpha_{j}\int_{0}^{1}\left(u-\mathbbm{1}\left(V_{i}\leq u\right)\right)\,\left(u-\mathbbm{1}\left(V_{j}\leq u\right)\right)\,du\,X_{i}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{X}}xx^{T}dP^{*}(x)\,X_{j}

where we have applied Tonelli’s theorem. Taking the expectation conditional on
\{w_{1:n},Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid w_{1:n},Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(u-\mathbbm{1}\left(V_{i}\leq u\right))^{2}\,du\,E[X_{i}^{T}\Sigma_{x}X_{i}\mid w_{1:n}]\,

where the cross-terms disappear as
V_{i}
is independent of
V_{j}
(and both are independent of
X_{i},X_{j}
) for
i\neq j
, and the terms have mean 0.
We can upper bound the above term by

\displaystyle E\left[X_{n+1}^{T}\Sigma_{x}X_{n+1}\mid w_{1:n}\right]\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}^{2}=O(n^{-1})\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}X_{i}^{T}\Sigma_{x}X_{i}\right]

where we have used the fact that
X_{n+1:\infty}
are i.i.d. conditional on
w_{1:n}
. Taking the expectation over the weights then gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:n}\right]\leq O(n^{-1})\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{T}\Sigma_{x}X_{i}\right]

As
\Sigma_{x}
has finite eigenvalues from Assumption
A4
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:\infty}\right]\leq O(n^{-1})\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{T}X_{i}\right)

We then have
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{T}X_{i}\to V^{2}<\infty
a.s., which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{d}^{2}_{2,x}(Q_{n\infty}(\cdot\mid x),Q_{n}(\cdot\mid x))\mid Y_{1:n},X_{1:\infty}\right]\leq O(n^{-1})\quad\text{a.s.}[P^{*}]

A similar argument as in the proof of Theorem
4
can be used to handle the second cross-term in (
A18
), so we have the result.
∎

We thus have posterior consistency of the QMP for linear regression for
\rho=1
.
A similar result can likely be derived for the posterior contraction rate. Like in the quantile estimation case, we suspect that the QMP with the smoothed update (
11
) satisfies a similar result on posterior consistency and contraction, but it is not immediately obvious due to the non-linearity of the increasing rearrangement operator. In practice, we see that the rearrangement is negligible for the initial estimate for a sequence
\rho_{N}
which approaches
1
sufficiently slowly. We leave a thorough investigation of this for future work.

Appendix D
Practical details

D.1
Implementation

In this section, we outline some computation details that were not mentioned in the main paper. All methods were implemented in the
JAX
package in Python, which is efficient and competitive with
C++
in terms of computational speed. The bivariate copula term
C_{\rho}(u,u^{\prime})
can be computed efficiently using standard approximations to the bivariate normal CDF; we utilize the implementation in
scipy
.

As the quantile function is scalar on bounded support
(0,1)
, it is efficient to implement methods based on a uniform discrete grid of size
n_{U}
. Rearrangement is particularly straightforward in this case, as it just involves sorting the values in increasing order
[
Chernozhukov et al., 2009
]
. For selecting
c
, we compute estimates of
q_{n}^{\dagger}
by taking finite differences of
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
