on the grid. We suspect it is possible to compute this more accurately using the derivatives of the update function but the rearrangement step makes it nontrivial. Finally, we outline the computational complexity of the main algorithms. Estimation of
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
(Algorithm
3
) has an average time complexity of
O(n\times n_{U}\log n_{U})
due to the sorting required for rearrangement, but in practice rearrangement is not required for each step.
Exact quantile predictive resampling (Algorithm
4
) has a time complexity of
O(B\times(N-n)\times n_{U})
, where
N
governs the truncation of predictive resampling. In practice, we select
N=n+5000
, although
N
can likely shrink with
n

[
Fong et al., 2023
]
. Approximate predictive resampling (Algorithm
5
) has time complexity
O(B\times n_{U}^{2})
, which is much faster in practice.
For all examples, we set the grid size to
n_{U}=200
, which does not need to grow with
n
. Finally, the algorithms for quantile regression (Algorithms
6
,
7
and
8
) have the same complexity as the original unconditional algorithms
multiplied by a factor of
p
.

D.2
Algorithms

In this section, we outline a few algorithms that were omitted from the main paper due to space constraints. Algorithm
8
outlines the exact quantile predictive resampling method for quantile regression, where we carry out the exact Bayesian bootstrap for the covariates for expediency.
Algorithm
9
then illustrates how to draw a sample from a GP with kernel
C_{\rho}(u,u^{\prime})-uu^{\prime}
on a finite grid of size
n_{U}
, which is essentially just equivalent to drawing a Gaussian vector. Algorithm
10
is then a natural extension to generate the GP from the covariate-dependent kernel, which we highlight is conditional on the randomly drawn
w_{1:n}
, so technically we would only want to draw
B=1
for each sample of
w_{1:n}
. This involves drawing
p
independent GPs and carrying out an affine transformation to induce the covariate dependence.

Algorithm 8

QMP sampling for regression

1

Initialize
\beta_{n}
from Algorithm
6

2

for

b\leftarrow 1

to

B

do

3

Draw
w_{1:n}^{(b)}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(1,\ldots,1)
and
X^{(b)}_{n+1:N}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{X_{i}}

4

for

i\leftarrow n+1

to

N

do

5

Draw
V^{(b)}_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)

6

\beta^{(b)}_{i}(u)=\beta^{(b)}_{i-1}(u)+\alpha_{i}\left[u-H_{\rho_{i}}\left(u,V^{(b)}_{i}\right)\right]\,X^{(b)}_{i}

7

end for

8

end for

9

Return
\left\{{\beta_{N}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\beta_{N}}^{(B)}\right\}

Algorithm 9

Sampling from GP with kernel
C_{\rho}(u,u^{\prime})-uu^{\prime}

1

Initialize uniform grid
{U}
on
[0,1]
of size
n_{U}

2

Compute the
n_{U}\times n_{U}
matrix
\Sigma
where
\Sigma_{ij}=C_{\rho}(u_{i},u_{j})-u_{i}u_{j}
for
(u_{i},u_{j})\in U\times U

3

Compute Cholesky decomposition
\Sigma=LL^{T}

4

for

b\leftarrow 1

to

B

do

5

Draw
Z^{(b)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n_{U}})

6

Compute
S^{(b)}=LZ^{(b)}

7

end for

8

Return
\left\{S^{(1)},\ldots,S^{(B)}\right\}

Algorithm 10

Sampling from GP with covariate-dependent kernel
k_{\rho}(\{u,j\},\{u^{\prime},j^{\prime}\};w_{1:n})

1

Initialize uniform grid
{U}
on
[0,1]
of size
n_{U}

2

Compute the
p\times p
matrix
\Sigma_{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}

3

Compute Cholesky decomposition
\Sigma_{x}=L_{x}{L_{x}}^{T}

4

for

b\leftarrow 1

to

B

do

5

Draw
Z_{1:p}^{(b)}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{GP}(0,C_{\rho^{2}}(u,u^{\prime})-uu^{\prime})
on grid
U\times U

6

Compute
S_{1:p}^{(b)}=L_{x}Z_{1:p}^{(b)}

7

end for

8

Return
\{S_{1:p}^{(1)},\ldots,S_{1:p}^{(B)}\}

n=50
.

E.3
Functional learning rates

As discussed in the main paper, we can consider a functional learning rate
a(u)
. It is not too difficult to extend Theorem
3
if
a(u)
satisfies the following.

Assumption A7

(Functional learning rate)
.

The learning rate sequence takes the form
\alpha_{i}(u)=a(u)(i+1)^{-1}
, where

L\leq a(u)\leq U
for some constants
0<L<U<\infty
.

Theorem A8
.

Under Assumptions
A7
,
4
and
5
, we have that
d_{2}(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*})\to 0
a.s.
[P^{*}]
under a variant of Algorithm
3
with a functional learning rate
a(u)
.

Proof.

Following the proof of Theorem
3
, let
L_{n}:=d_{2}^{2}(Q_{n}^{\dagger},Q^{*})
. We once again have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]\leq L_{n-1}-2(n+1)^{-1}\widetilde{T}(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger})+U^{2}(n+1)^{-2}+2(n+1)^{-1}\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}

(A19)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\int a(u)\left(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)\right)\left(u-P^{*}\left(Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)\right)\right)\,du

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\,a(u)\,\left(Q^{*}(u)-Q^{\dagger}_{n-1}(u)\right)\left(K_{n}(u)-P^{*}\left(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}(u)\right)\right)du

and
K_{n}(u)=\int H_{\rho_{n}}(u,P_{n-1}(y))\,p^{*}(y)\,dy
as before.
Once again, we have
\widetilde{T}(Q)\geq 0
as the integrand is always positive. We can upper bound
|\zeta_{n}|
again with

\displaystyle|\zeta_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{L_{n-1}}\sqrt{\int a^{2}(u)\left(K_{n}(u)-P^{*}\left(Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}(u)\right)\right)^{2}\,du}

\displaystyle\leq(L_{n-1}+1)\,U\sqrt{\kappa_{n}},

where
\kappa_{n}
is defined in (
A11
). It is thus again sufficient to show
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(i+1)^{-1}\sqrt{\kappa_{i}}<\infty
which occurs under the same assumptions as before (i.e. Assumption
4
).

Once again, we have
L_{n}\to L_{\infty}
a.s. and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(i+1)^{-1}\widetilde{T}(Q^{\dagger}_{i-1})<\infty
a.s. from Theorem
A4
. As the integrand in
\widetilde{T}(Q)
is positive, we can further lower bound

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}(Q)\geq LT(Q),

so we also have
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(i+1)^{-1}{T}(Q^{\dagger}_{i-1})<\infty
a.s. The same argument based on the Lipschitz constant can then be applied to show
L_{\infty}=0
a.s.
∎

In practice, an intuitive choice for the functional learning rate is to set

\displaystyle a(u)=\frac{1}{\hat{p}(\hat{Q}(u))},

where
\hat{p}
and
\hat{Q}
are estimates of
p^{*}
and
Q^{*}
respectively. This can be motivated by optimal learning rates for attaining efficient stochastic approximation of pointwise quantiles which is also suggested by
Aboubacar and Thiam [2014]
. In the quantile regression case, under appropriate assumptions, this would involve estimating the residuals via linear regression, then estimating
\hat{p}
and
\hat{Q}
from the residuals. As discussed in the main paper however, it is unsatisfying that a separate density estimate is required, and the results will also be quite sensitive to this density estimate.

Figure
A5
illustrates the same experiment as Section
7.1
but instead with
a(u)
as above, where we estimate
\hat{p}
and
\hat{Q}
with the Gaussian kernel density estimate and empirical quantile function respectively. We set
c=0.7
to match the settings of the main paper, with all other settings the same. We can see that the center and tails have slightly less and more uncertainty respectively compared to the main paper,
due to the adaptive
a(u)
. However, the estimates and intervals are quite non-smooth despite setting a large value of
c=0.7
.
This suggests that while an adaptive
a(u)
may help with estimating
Q^{*}
, it may not be better for estimating the quantile density function
q^{*}
. Finally, we see that the posterior of the mean functional looks quite similar to the fixed
a
result.

Figure A5:
QMP over
Q_{\infty}^{\dagger}
with functional learning rate
a(u)
for (Left)
n=50
; (Middle)
n=500
; (Right) QMP over
\theta=\mathbb{E}[Y]
; we only show the GP approximation as it is visually indistinguishable from exact sampling.

E.4
Comparison to the Bayesian bootstrap for quantile regression

We now draw comparisons between the QMP for quantile regression with
\rho=1
with the Bayesian bootstrap.
Consider now a new test point
x
, which is distinct from
X_{1:n}
. Under the BB, the posterior distribution over
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid x]
is always 0 in this case, as the BB only allocates mass to
x=X_{i}
. However, the QMP will be the distribution of

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\infty}[Y\mid x]=\int Q^{\dagger}_{\infty}(u\mid x)\,du

\displaystyle=\int Q_{\infty}(u\mid x)\,du=x^{T}\int\beta_{\infty}(u)\,du.

This is thus non-zero for all values of
x
. The QMP thus allows posterior inference on
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid x]
for the whole covariate space, which the Bayesian bootstrap is unable to do. Of course the same argument also applies if we are interested in the posterior over
Q(u\mid x)
for some
x
not in the support of the data.
